Mostly outside things, I think. Or… outside things start it, at least. Like if I’m on a grant call or in a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or one of the students will make some side comment, or I’ll see some odd peak in an old spectrum, and then my brain just latches onto it.

Not that I’m bored with the experiment—or at least I don’t think so. I’ll fully intend to stay with the main task, and then some little detail catches me, like a pump sounding slightly off, or a comment someone makes, or a weird peak in data I wasn’t even supposed to be reviewing right then.\n\nAnd then I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I guess… but suddenly I’ve spent hours on that, and the actual thing I was responsible for has slipped. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these distractions usually triggered by things outside of you, like what you see or hear, or by your own thoughts and ideas?\n\nPatient: Mostly outside things, I think. Or… outside things start it, at least. Like if I’m on a grant call or in a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or one of the students will make some side comment, or I’ll see some odd peak in an old spectrum, and then my brain just latches onto it.\n\nI tell myself it’s relevant, like I’m being careful, but then I’m off chasing that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people were waiting on from me just… doesn’t get done properly. I know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it’s just what keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly active or feeling like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, sometimes. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it acting without thinking… I mean, I do try to be responsible. Maybe I make decisions quickly sometimes, especially at work, but I usually have some reason in my head at the time.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what you mean by impulsive. Yes… I think that’s when it’s worse. When I’m in that high-energy mode, it feels like my mind is moving fast enough that every little thing seems important.

I’ll chase that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people needed from me kind of falls apart.\n\nI know that probably sounds like poor prioritizing, and I’m trying not to overstate it, but it’s become enough of a problem that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble meeting your responsibilities at work because of this?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, yes. I mean, I’ve been trying to frame it as just needing better systems, but it’s gotten past that.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself because I’ve missed priorities, mixed up scheduling, and I think my supervision has felt… less steady. People have been kind about it, mostly, but it’s started affecting the project, and there have been concerns about meeting lab safety expectations. I’m embarrassed saying that, but yes, it’s interfering with my responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it restless, exactly. I don’t think I’m usually tapping my foot or pacing or anything obvious like that. It’s more that my attention sort of slips away. I can sit still, I think… at least I hope I’m not visibly fidgeting in meetings. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're getting distracted by other things, or is it just that you lose interest in what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I get pulled by other things, honestly. Not that I’m bored with the experiment—or at least I don’t think so. I’ll fully intend to stay with the main task, and then some little detail catches me, like a pump sounding slightly off, or a comment someone makes, or a weird peak in data I wasn’t even supposed to be reviewing right then.\n\nAnd then I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I guess… but suddenly I’ve spent hours on that, and the actual thing I was responsible for has slipped. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these distractions usually triggered by things outside of you, like what you see or hear, or by your own thoughts and ideas?\n\nPatient: Mostly outside things, I think. Or… outside things start it, at least. Like if I’m on a grant call or in a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or one of the students will make some side comment, or I’ll see some odd peak in an old spectrum, and then my brain just latches onto it.\n\nI tell myself it’s relevant, like I’m being careful, but then I’m off chasing that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people were waiting on from me just… doesn’t get done properly. I know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it’s just what keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly active or feeling like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I do get a lot done when I’m like that. But there are periods where I’m at the lab before anyone else gets there, and I’m rewriting protocols, rearranging the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in different countries, and starting new analyses all at once.\n\nIt feels productive at the time—like sitting still would be wasting this one chance to fix everything, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it acting without thinking… I mean, I do try to be responsible. Maybe I make decisions quickly sometimes, especially at work, but I usually have some reason in my head at the time.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what you mean by impulsive. Like, do you mean careless, or just moving too fast? I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're more prone to getting distracted when you're in a state of high energy or activity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s when it’s worse. When I’m in that high-energy mode, it feels like my mind is moving fast enough that every little thing seems important.\n\nLike I can be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student makes some offhand comment, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m following that instead. At the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, but then hours have gone by and the actual experiment, or the thing people needed from me, has sort of fallen apart.\n\nI don’t mean to sound irresponsible. It’s just… yes, it does seem tied to when I’m more revved up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I feel… pretty anxious, honestly. I try not to show it, because I know everyone is busy and I don’t want to seem like I can’t handle my role.

I’ll chase that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people needed from me kind of falls apart.\n\nI know that probably sounds like poor prioritizing, and I’m trying not to overstate it, but it’s become enough of a problem that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble meeting your responsibilities at work because of this?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, yes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it is related. When I’m in that more elevated state—if that’s the right word—it almost feels like the lab is finally moving at my speed. Everything feels charged, like there are connections everywhere and I can see what needs to happen.

I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just being difficult, but… when someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I can get irritated. More than I’d like. I’ve snapped at people, and I think some of the students have started avoiding my office because of it.

That’s hard to say, because I don’t want to be that kind of person. But yes, the distraction and losing focus seem worse when I’m in that energized, keyed-up mood.

I’ll chase that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people needed from me kind of falls apart.\n\nI know that probably sounds like poor prioritizing, and I’m trying not to overstate it, but it’s become enough of a problem that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble meeting your responsibilities at work because of this?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, yes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… mostly noticeable at work, if I’m being honest. That’s where it becomes obvious because there are meetings and experiments and people depending on me.

I don’t want to overstate it, but even when it’s not exactly a deadline, like during a grant call or a safety meeting, my attention can just jump. I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m following that thread for hours.

At the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, if that makes sense. But then the actual experiment, or the thing everyone needed from me, has sort of… fallen apart in the background. That’s really the problem.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I’m here because I’m having a really hard time staying on the thing I’m supposed to be doing at work. I’m a scientist, and it’s gotten to where in the middle of a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice something small—like a pump vibrating strangely, or a student making an offhand comment, or some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m just… gone. I’ll chase that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people needed from me kind of falls apart.\n\nI know that probably sounds like poor prioritizing, and I’m trying not to overstate it, but it’s become enough of a problem that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble meeting your responsibilities at work because of this?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, yes. I mean, I’ve been trying to frame it as just needing better systems, but it’s gotten past that.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself because I’ve missed priorities, mixed up scheduling, and I think my supervision has felt… less steady. People have been kind about it, mostly, but it’s started affecting the project, and there have been concerns about meeting lab safety expectations. I’m embarrassed saying that, but yes, it’s interfering with my responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it restless, exactly. I don’t think I’m usually tapping my foot or pacing or anything obvious like that. It’s more that my attention sort of slips away. I can sit still, I think… at least I hope I’m not visibly fidgeting in meetings. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're getting distracted by other things, or is it just that you lose interest in what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I get pulled by other things, honestly. Not that I’m bored with the experiment—or at least I don’t think so. I’ll fully intend to stay with the main task, and then some little detail catches me, like a pump sounding slightly off, or a comment someone makes, or a weird peak in data I wasn’t even supposed to be reviewing right then.\n\nAnd then I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I guess… but suddenly I’ve spent hours on that, and the actual thing I was responsible for has slipped. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these distractions usually triggered by things outside of you, like what you see or hear, or by your own thoughts and ideas?\n\nPatient: Mostly outside things, I think. Or… outside things start it, at least. Like if I’m on a grant call or in a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or one of the students will make some side comment, or I’ll see some odd peak in an old spectrum, and then my brain just latches onto it.\n\nI tell myself it’s relevant, like I’m being careful, but then I’m off chasing that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people were waiting on from me just… doesn’t get done properly. I know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it’s just what keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly active or feeling like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I do get a lot done when I’m like that. But there are periods where I’m at the lab before anyone else gets there, and I’m rewriting protocols, rearranging the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in different countries, and starting new analyses all at once.\n\nIt feels productive at the time—like sitting still would be wasting this one chance to fix everything, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it acting without thinking… I mean, I do try to be responsible. Maybe I make decisions quickly sometimes, especially at work, but I usually have some reason in my head at the time.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what you mean by impulsive. Like, do you mean careless, or just moving too fast? I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're more prone to getting distracted when you're in a state of high energy or activity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s when it’s worse. When I’m in that high-energy mode, it feels like my mind is moving fast enough that every little thing seems important.\n\nLike I can be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student makes some offhand comment, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m following that instead. At the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, but then hours have gone by and the actual experiment, or the thing people needed from me, has sort of fallen apart.\n\nI don’t mean to sound irresponsible. It’s just… yes, it does seem tied to when I’m more revved up.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you realize you've gotten off track and aren't meeting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I feel… pretty anxious, honestly. I try not to show it, because I know everyone is busy and I don’t want to seem like I can’t handle my role.\n\nBut when I realize I’ve gone down the wrong path or missed what actually needed attention, it hits hard. My department has had to step in on some decisions I used to manage myself—scheduling, priorities, supervision things. I’d like to say it was just extra oversight, but… it was because things were getting confused, and there were concerns about the project and even the lab safety expectations.\n\nThat’s embarrassing to admit. I do care about the work. So yes, when I see that happening, I get very distressed. I worry I’ve let people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get distracted and have trouble focusing in your personal life as well, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly at work, I think… or at least that’s where it causes the most trouble, because the stakes are higher and people are waiting on me.\n\nAt home I can get a little scattered, but what really made me come in was that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Like I’m following an important lead. But then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. That’s… that’s the part that worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your tendency to get distracted and lose focus is related to your mood or emotional state at the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is related. When I’m in that more elevated state—if that’s the right word—it almost feels like the lab is finally moving at my speed. I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m following that thread for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, if that makes sense. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, when I’m completely away from work, it’s harder for me to tell. I don’t think it shows up as dramatically on vacation, because there isn’t an experiment or a room full of people waiting for me to stay on track.

What made me come in is really what happens at work—like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch on to something small, a pump vibration or a student’s offhand comment or even some old spectrum peak I suddenly remember, and then I’m off following that for hours. It feels important in the moment, not like I’m just drifting… but then the actual thing I was supposed to be focusing on has fallen apart.

So, I guess away from work it may happen in smaller ways, but I notice it most when there’s something concrete I’ve let slip. Does that make sense?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I’m here because I’m having a really hard time staying on the thing I’m supposed to be doing at work. I’m a scientist, and it’s gotten to where in the middle of a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice something small—like a pump vibrating strangely, or a student making an offhand comment, or some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m just… gone. I’ll chase that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people needed from me kind of falls apart.\n\nI know that probably sounds like poor prioritizing, and I’m trying not to overstate it, but it’s become enough of a problem that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble meeting your responsibilities at work because of this?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, yes. I mean, I’ve been trying to frame it as just needing better systems, but it’s gotten past that.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself because I’ve missed priorities, mixed up scheduling, and I think my supervision has felt… less steady. People have been kind about it, mostly, but it’s started affecting the project, and there have been concerns about meeting lab safety expectations. I’m embarrassed saying that, but yes, it’s interfering with my responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it restless, exactly. I don’t think I’m usually tapping my foot or pacing or anything obvious like that. It’s more that my attention sort of slips away. I can sit still, I think… at least I hope I’m not visibly fidgeting in meetings. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're getting distracted by other things, or is it just that you lose interest in what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I get pulled by other things, honestly. Not that I’m bored with the experiment—or at least I don’t think so. I’ll fully intend to stay with the main task, and then some little detail catches me, like a pump sounding slightly off, or a comment someone makes, or a weird peak in data I wasn’t even supposed to be reviewing right then.\n\nAnd then I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I guess… but suddenly I’ve spent hours on that, and the actual thing I was responsible for has slipped. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these distractions usually triggered by things outside of you, like what you see or hear, or by your own thoughts and ideas?\n\nPatient: Mostly outside things, I think. Or… outside things start it, at least. Like if I’m on a grant call or in a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or one of the students will make some side comment, or I’ll see some odd peak in an old spectrum, and then my brain just latches onto it.\n\nI tell myself it’s relevant, like I’m being careful, but then I’m off chasing that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people were waiting on from me just… doesn’t get done properly. I know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it’s just what keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly active or feeling like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I do get a lot done when I’m like that. But there are periods where I’m at the lab before anyone else gets there, and I’m rewriting protocols, rearranging the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in different countries, and starting new analyses all at once.\n\nIt feels productive at the time—like sitting still would be wasting this one chance to fix everything, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it acting without thinking… I mean, I do try to be responsible. Maybe I make decisions quickly sometimes, especially at work, but I usually have some reason in my head at the time.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what you mean by impulsive. Like, do you mean careless, or just moving too fast? I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're more prone to getting distracted when you're in a state of high energy or activity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s when it’s worse. When I’m in that high-energy mode, it feels like my mind is moving fast enough that every little thing seems important.\n\nLike I can be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student makes some offhand comment, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m following that instead. At the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, but then hours have gone by and the actual experiment, or the thing people needed from me, has sort of fallen apart.\n\nI don’t mean to sound irresponsible. It’s just… yes, it does seem tied to when I’m more revved up.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you realize you've gotten off track and aren't meeting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I feel… pretty anxious, honestly. I try not to show it, because I know everyone is busy and I don’t want to seem like I can’t handle my role.\n\nBut when I realize I’ve gone down the wrong path or missed what actually needed attention, it hits hard. My department has had to step in on some decisions I used to manage myself—scheduling, priorities, supervision things. I’d like to say it was just extra oversight, but… it was because things were getting confused, and there were concerns about the project and even the lab safety expectations.\n\nThat’s embarrassing to admit. I do care about the work. So yes, when I see that happening, I get very distressed. I worry I’ve let people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get distracted and have trouble focusing in your personal life as well, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly at work, I think… or at least that’s where it causes the most trouble, because the stakes are higher and people are waiting on me.\n\nAt home I can get a little scattered, but what really made me come in was that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I notice something like a pump vibration, or a student says something in passing, or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being diligent, you know? Like I’m following an important lead. But then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. That’s… that’s the part that worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your tendency to get distracted and lose focus is related to your mood or emotional state at the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is related. When I’m in that more elevated state—if that’s the right word—it almost feels like the lab is finally moving at my speed. Everything feels charged, like there are connections everywhere and I can see what needs to happen.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just being difficult, but… when someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I can get irritated. I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m following that thread for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, if that makes sense. But then the actual experiment, or the thing everyone needed from me, has sort of… fallen apart in the background. But there are periods where I’m at the lab before anyone else gets there, and I’m rewriting protocols, rearranging the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in different countries, and starting new analyses all at once.\n\nIt feels productive at the time—like sitting still would be wasting this one chance to fix everything, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it acting without thinking… I mean, I do try to be responsible. Maybe I make decisions quickly sometimes, especially at work, but I usually have some reason in my head at the time.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what you mean by impulsive. Like, do you mean careless, or just moving too fast? I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're more prone to getting distracted when you're in a state of high energy or activity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s when it’s worse. When I’m in that high-energy mode, it feels like my mind is moving fast enough that every little thing seems important.\n\nLike I can be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student makes some offhand comment, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m following that instead. At the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, but then hours have gone by and the actual experiment, or the thing people needed from me, has sort of fallen apart.\n\nI don’t mean to sound irresponsible. It’s just… yes, it does seem tied to when I’m more revved up.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you realize you've gotten off track and aren't meeting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I feel… pretty anxious, honestly. I try not to show it, because I know everyone is busy and I don’t want to seem like I can’t handle my role.\n\nBut when I realize I’ve gone down the wrong path or missed what actually needed attention, it hits hard. My department has had to step in on some decisions I used to manage myself—scheduling, priorities, supervision things. I’d like to say it was just extra oversight, but… it was because things were getting confused, and there were concerns about the project and even the lab safety expectations.\n\nThat’s embarrassing to admit. I do care about the work. So yes, when I see that happening, I get very distressed. I worry I’ve let people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get distracted and have trouble focusing in your personal life as well, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly at work, I think… or at least that’s where it causes the most trouble, because the stakes are higher and people are waiting on me.\n\nAt home I can get a little scattered, but what really made me come in was that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I notice something like a pump vibration, or a student says something in passing, or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being diligent, you know? Like I’m following an important lead. But then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. That’s… that’s the part that worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your tendency to get distracted and lose focus is related to your mood or emotional state at the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is related. When I’m in that more elevated state—if that’s the right word—it almost feels like the lab is finally moving at my speed. Everything feels charged, like there are connections everywhere and I can see what needs to happen.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just being difficult, but… when someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I can get irritated. More than I’d like. I’ve snapped at people, and I think some of the students have started avoiding my office because of it.\n\nThat’s hard to say, because I don’t want to be that kind of person. But yes, the distraction and losing focus seem worse when I’m in that energized, keyed-up mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with distraction and focus at times when you're not at work or under pressure to meet deadlines?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly noticeable at work, if I’m being honest. That’s where it becomes obvious because there are meetings and experiments and people depending on me.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but even when it’s not exactly a deadline, like during a grant call or a safety meeting, my attention can just jump. I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m following that thread for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, if that makes sense. But then the actual experiment, or the thing everyone needed from me, has sort of… fallen apart in the background. That’s really the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulties with distraction and focus when you are on vacation or completely away from work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Honestly, when I’m completely away from work, it’s harder for me to tell. I don’t think it shows up as dramatically on vacation, because there isn’t an experiment or a room full of people waiting for me to stay on track.\n\nWhat made me come in is really what happens at work—like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch on to something small, a pump vibration or a student’s offhand comment or even some old spectrum peak I suddenly remember, and then I’m off following that for hours. It feels important in the moment, not like I’m just drifting… but then the actual thing I was supposed to be focusing on has fallen apart.\n\nSo, I guess away from work it may happen in smaller ways, but I notice it most when there’s something concrete I’ve let slip. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’d like to think a different environment would help… and maybe it would, if there were fewer interruptions or less pressure. But I’m not sure it would go away completely.

I think… yes, probably. I wish I could say it only happens when I’m bored or stressed, but that wouldn’t be completely honest.

I’m not trying to make excuses—it’s just what keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly active or feeling like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I do get a lot done when I’m like that. But there are periods where I’m at the lab before anyone else gets there, and I’m rewriting protocols, rearranging the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in different countries, and starting new analyses all at once.\n\nIt feels productive at the time—like sitting still would be wasting this one chance to fix everything, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it acting without thinking… I mean, I do try to be responsible. Maybe I make decisions quickly sometimes, especially at work, but I usually have some reason in my head at the time.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what you mean by impulsive. Like, do you mean careless, or just moving too fast? I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're more prone to getting distracted when you're in a state of high energy or activity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s when it’s worse. When I’m in that high-energy mode, it feels like my mind is moving fast enough that every little thing seems important.\n\nLike I can be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student makes some offhand comment, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m following that instead. At the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, but then hours have gone by and the actual experiment, or the thing people needed from me, has sort of fallen apart.\n\nI don’t mean to sound irresponsible. It’s just… yes, it does seem tied to when I’m more revved up.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you realize you've gotten off track and aren't meeting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I feel… pretty anxious, honestly. I try not to show it, because I know everyone is busy and I don’t want to seem like I can’t handle my role.\n\nBut when I realize I’ve gone down the wrong path or missed what actually needed attention, it hits hard. My department has had to step in on some decisions I used to manage myself—scheduling, priorities, supervision things. I’d like to say it was just extra oversight, but… it was because things were getting confused, and there were concerns about the project and even the lab safety expectations.\n\nThat’s embarrassing to admit. I do care about the work. So yes, when I see that happening, I get very distressed. I worry I’ve let people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get distracted and have trouble focusing in your personal life as well, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly at work, I think… or at least that’s where it causes the most trouble, because the stakes are higher and people are waiting on me.\n\nAt home I can get a little scattered, but what really made me come in was that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I notice something like a pump vibration, or a student says something in passing, or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being diligent, you know? Like I’m following an important lead. But then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. That’s… that’s the part that worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your tendency to get distracted and lose focus is related to your mood or emotional state at the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is related. When I’m in that more elevated state—if that’s the right word—it almost feels like the lab is finally moving at my speed. Everything feels charged, like there are connections everywhere and I can see what needs to happen.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just being difficult, but… when someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I can get irritated. More than I’d like. I’ve snapped at people, and I think some of the students have started avoiding my office because of it.\n\nThat’s hard to say, because I don’t want to be that kind of person. But yes, the distraction and losing focus seem worse when I’m in that energized, keyed-up mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with distraction and focus at times when you're not at work or under pressure to meet deadlines?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly noticeable at work, if I’m being honest. That’s where it becomes obvious because there are meetings and experiments and people depending on me.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but even when it’s not exactly a deadline, like during a grant call or a safety meeting, my attention can just jump. I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m following that thread for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, if that makes sense. But then the actual experiment, or the thing everyone needed from me, has sort of… fallen apart in the background. That’s really the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulties with distraction and focus when you are on vacation or completely away from work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Honestly, when I’m completely away from work, it’s harder for me to tell. I don’t think it shows up as dramatically on vacation, because there isn’t an experiment or a room full of people waiting for me to stay on track.\n\nWhat made me come in is really what happens at work—like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch on to something small, a pump vibration or a student’s offhand comment or even some old spectrum peak I suddenly remember, and then I’m off following that for hours. It feels important in the moment, not like I’m just drifting… but then the actual thing I was supposed to be focusing on has fallen apart.\n\nSo, I guess away from work it may happen in smaller ways, but I notice it most when there’s something concrete I’ve let slip. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have these problems with distraction and focus if you were to change jobs or work environments?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think a different environment would help… and maybe it would, if there were fewer interruptions or less pressure. But I’m not sure it would go away completely.\n\nWhat worries me is that it isn’t just this one job setup. I came in because I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Even in something structured, like a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum, and then I’m off chasing that for hours.\n\nIn the moment it feels relevant—like I’m being careful, not careless—but then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. So… I don’t know. I think changing jobs might make it less obvious, but I’m afraid the pattern would follow me. Does that sound reasonable?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with distraction and focus would still be present even if you were doing something you really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I wish I could say it only happens when I’m bored or stressed, but that wouldn’t be completely honest.\n\nA lot of the work I’m talking about is work I actually do enjoy. I like the experiments, I like noticing patterns, I like thinking through odd results. But that’s almost part of the problem — during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch on to something like a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll remember some strange peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nIn my head it feels productive, like I’m following something important. But then the actual experiment, or the thing people needed me to stay with, has fallen apart. So… yes, I think it would still be there, even with something I care about. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Even in something structured, like a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum, and then I’m off chasing that for hours.\n\nIn the moment it feels relevant—like I’m being careful, not careless—but then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. So… I don’t know. I think changing jobs might make it less obvious, but I’m afraid the pattern would follow me. Does that sound reasonable?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with distraction and focus would still be present even if you were doing something you really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I wish I could say it only happens when I’m bored or stressed, but that wouldn’t be completely honest.\n\nA lot of the work I’m talking about is work I actually do enjoy. I like the experiments, I like noticing patterns, I like thinking through odd results. But that’s almost part of the problem — during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch on to something like a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll remember some strange peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nIn my head it feels productive, like I’m following something important. But then the actual experiment, or the thing people needed me to stay with, has fallen apart. So… yes, I think it would still be there, even with something I care about. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any effects of your distraction and focus issues on your personal relationships or friendships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 42 / physical_scientist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle because missed priorities, confused scheduling, and strained supervision started putting the project and lab safety expectations at risk.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… personally, I’ve tried to keep it from spilling over too much. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming my relationships for work problems, or anything like that.

But I guess the honest answer is that it has affected people around me, especially at work. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle on my own, because I was missing priorities, mixing up scheduling, and not supervising as clearly as I should have. It got to the point where people were worried about the project and even the lab safety expectations.

That’s… embarrassing to say. I know you asked about friendships and relationships, and I’m not sure I have a neat answer there, but professionally, the relationships have definitely been strained. I don’t think people are trying to be unkind; I think they just can’t rely on me the same way right now. Does that make sense?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I’m here because I’m having a really hard time staying on the thing I’m supposed to be doing at work. I’m a scientist, and it’s gotten to where in the middle of a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice something small—like a pump vibrating strangely, or a student making an offhand comment, or some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m just… gone. I’ll chase that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people needed from me kind of falls apart.\n\nI know that probably sounds like poor prioritizing, and I’m trying not to overstate it, but it’s become enough of a problem that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble meeting your responsibilities at work because of this?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, yes. I mean, I’ve been trying to frame it as just needing better systems, but it’s gotten past that.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself because I’ve missed priorities, mixed up scheduling, and I think my supervision has felt… less steady. People have been kind about it, mostly, but it’s started affecting the project, and there have been concerns about meeting lab safety expectations. I’m embarrassed saying that, but yes, it’s interfering with my responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it restless, exactly. I don’t think I’m usually tapping my foot or pacing or anything obvious like that. It’s more that my attention sort of slips away. I can sit still, I think… at least I hope I’m not visibly fidgeting in meetings. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're getting distracted by other things, or is it just that you lose interest in what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I get pulled by other things, honestly. Not that I’m bored with the experiment—or at least I don’t think so. I’ll fully intend to stay with the main task, and then some little detail catches me, like a pump sounding slightly off, or a comment someone makes, or a weird peak in data I wasn’t even supposed to be reviewing right then.\n\nAnd then I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I guess… but suddenly I’ve spent hours on that, and the actual thing I was responsible for has slipped. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these distractions usually triggered by things outside of you, like what you see or hear, or by your own thoughts and ideas?\n\nPatient: Mostly outside things, I think. Or… outside things start it, at least. Like if I’m on a grant call or in a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or one of the students will make some side comment, or I’ll see some odd peak in an old spectrum, and then my brain just latches onto it.\n\nI tell myself it’s relevant, like I’m being careful, but then I’m off chasing that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people were waiting on from me just… doesn’t get done properly. I know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it’s just what keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly active or feeling like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I do get a lot done when I’m like that. But there are periods where I’m at the lab before anyone else gets there, and I’m rewriting protocols, rearranging the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in different countries, and starting new analyses all at once.\n\nIt feels productive at the time—like sitting still would be wasting this one chance to fix everything, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it acting without thinking… I mean, I do try to be responsible. Maybe I make decisions quickly sometimes, especially at work, but I usually have some reason in my head at the time.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what you mean by impulsive. Like, do you mean careless, or just moving too fast? I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're more prone to getting distracted when you're in a state of high energy or activity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s when it’s worse. When I’m in that high-energy mode, it feels like my mind is moving fast enough that every little thing seems important.\n\nLike I can be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student makes some offhand comment, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m following that instead. At the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, but then hours have gone by and the actual experiment, or the thing people needed from me, has sort of fallen apart.\n\nI don’t mean to sound irresponsible. It’s just… yes, it does seem tied to when I’m more revved up.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you realize you've gotten off track and aren't meeting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I feel… pretty anxious, honestly. I try not to show it, because I know everyone is busy and I don’t want to seem like I can’t handle my role.\n\nBut when I realize I’ve gone down the wrong path or missed what actually needed attention, it hits hard. My department has had to step in on some decisions I used to manage myself—scheduling, priorities, supervision things. I’d like to say it was just extra oversight, but… it was because things were getting confused, and there were concerns about the project and even the lab safety expectations.\n\nThat’s embarrassing to admit. I do care about the work. So yes, when I see that happening, I get very distressed. I worry I’ve let people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get distracted and have trouble focusing in your personal life as well, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly at work, I think… or at least that’s where it causes the most trouble, because the stakes are higher and people are waiting on me.\n\nAt home I can get a little scattered, but what really made me come in was that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I notice something like a pump vibration, or a student says something in passing, or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being diligent, you know? Like I’m following an important lead. But then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. That’s… that’s the part that worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your tendency to get distracted and lose focus is related to your mood or emotional state at the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is related. When I’m in that more elevated state—if that’s the right word—it almost feels like the lab is finally moving at my speed. Everything feels charged, like there are connections everywhere and I can see what needs to happen.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just being difficult, but… when someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I can get irritated. More than I’d like. I’ve snapped at people, and I think some of the students have started avoiding my office because of it.\n\nThat’s hard to say, because I don’t want to be that kind of person. But yes, the distraction and losing focus seem worse when I’m in that energized, keyed-up mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with distraction and focus at times when you're not at work or under pressure to meet deadlines?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly noticeable at work, if I’m being honest. That’s where it becomes obvious because there are meetings and experiments and people depending on me.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but even when it’s not exactly a deadline, like during a grant call or a safety meeting, my attention can just jump. I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m following that thread for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, if that makes sense. But then the actual experiment, or the thing everyone needed from me, has sort of… fallen apart in the background. That’s really the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulties with distraction and focus when you are on vacation or completely away from work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Honestly, when I’m completely away from work, it’s harder for me to tell. I don’t think it shows up as dramatically on vacation, because there isn’t an experiment or a room full of people waiting for me to stay on track.\n\nWhat made me come in is really what happens at work—like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch on to something small, a pump vibration or a student’s offhand comment or even some old spectrum peak I suddenly remember, and then I’m off following that for hours. It feels important in the moment, not like I’m just drifting… but then the actual thing I was supposed to be focusing on has fallen apart.\n\nSo, I guess away from work it may happen in smaller ways, but I notice it most when there’s something concrete I’ve let slip. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have these problems with distraction and focus if you were to change jobs or work environments?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think a different environment would help… and maybe it would, if there were fewer interruptions or less pressure. But I’m not sure it would go away completely.\n\nWhat worries me is that it isn’t just this one job setup. I came in because I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Even in something structured, like a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum, and then I’m off chasing that for hours.\n\nIn the moment it feels relevant—like I’m being careful, not careless—but then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. So… I don’t know. I think changing jobs might make it less obvious, but I’m afraid the pattern would follow me. I’d like to say I’m managing okay outside of work, and in some ways I try to keep things looking normal. But… I think the clearest place it’s showing up is still work, because that’s where the consequences are harder to hide.

People have been kind about it, mostly, but it’s started affecting the project, and there have been concerns about meeting lab safety expectations. I’m embarrassed saying that, but yes, it’s interfering with my responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it restless, exactly. I don’t think I’m usually tapping my foot or pacing or anything obvious like that. It’s more that my attention sort of slips away. I can sit still, I think… at least I hope I’m not visibly fidgeting in meetings. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're getting distracted by other things, or is it just that you lose interest in what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I get pulled by other things, honestly. Not that I’m bored with the experiment—or at least I don’t think so. I’ll fully intend to stay with the main task, and then some little detail catches me, like a pump sounding slightly off, or a comment someone makes, or a weird peak in data I wasn’t even supposed to be reviewing right then.\n\nAnd then I tell myself I’m just being thorough, which sounds better, I guess… but suddenly I’ve spent hours on that, and the actual thing I was responsible for has slipped. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these distractions usually triggered by things outside of you, like what you see or hear, or by your own thoughts and ideas?\n\nPatient: Mostly outside things, I think. Or… outside things start it, at least. Like if I’m on a grant call or in a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or one of the students will make some side comment, or I’ll see some odd peak in an old spectrum, and then my brain just latches onto it.\n\nI tell myself it’s relevant, like I’m being careful, but then I’m off chasing that for hours, and the actual experiment or the thing people were waiting on from me just… doesn’t get done properly. I know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it’s just what keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly active or feeling like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I do get a lot done when I’m like that. But there are periods where I’m at the lab before anyone else gets there, and I’m rewriting protocols, rearranging the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in different countries, and starting new analyses all at once.\n\nIt feels productive at the time—like sitting still would be wasting this one chance to fix everything, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it acting without thinking… I mean, I do try to be responsible. Maybe I make decisions quickly sometimes, especially at work, but I usually have some reason in my head at the time.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what you mean by impulsive. Like, do you mean careless, or just moving too fast? I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're more prone to getting distracted when you're in a state of high energy or activity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s when it’s worse. When I’m in that high-energy mode, it feels like my mind is moving fast enough that every little thing seems important.\n\nLike I can be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student makes some offhand comment, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m following that instead. At the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, but then hours have gone by and the actual experiment, or the thing people needed from me, has sort of fallen apart.\n\nI don’t mean to sound irresponsible. It’s just… yes, it does seem tied to when I’m more revved up.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you realize you've gotten off track and aren't meeting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I feel… pretty anxious, honestly. I try not to show it, because I know everyone is busy and I don’t want to seem like I can’t handle my role.\n\nBut when I realize I’ve gone down the wrong path or missed what actually needed attention, it hits hard. My department has had to step in on some decisions I used to manage myself—scheduling, priorities, supervision things. I’d like to say it was just extra oversight, but… it was because things were getting confused, and there were concerns about the project and even the lab safety expectations.\n\nThat’s embarrassing to admit. I do care about the work. So yes, when I see that happening, I get very distressed. I worry I’ve let people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get distracted and have trouble focusing in your personal life as well, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly at work, I think… or at least that’s where it causes the most trouble, because the stakes are higher and people are waiting on me.\n\nAt home I can get a little scattered, but what really made me come in was that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I notice something like a pump vibration, or a student says something in passing, or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being diligent, you know? Like I’m following an important lead. But then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. That’s… that’s the part that worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your tendency to get distracted and lose focus is related to your mood or emotional state at the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is related. When I’m in that more elevated state—if that’s the right word—it almost feels like the lab is finally moving at my speed. Everything feels charged, like there are connections everywhere and I can see what needs to happen.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just being difficult, but… when someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I can get irritated. More than I’d like. I’ve snapped at people, and I think some of the students have started avoiding my office because of it.\n\nThat’s hard to say, because I don’t want to be that kind of person. But yes, the distraction and losing focus seem worse when I’m in that energized, keyed-up mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with distraction and focus at times when you're not at work or under pressure to meet deadlines?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly noticeable at work, if I’m being honest. That’s where it becomes obvious because there are meetings and experiments and people depending on me.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but even when it’s not exactly a deadline, like during a grant call or a safety meeting, my attention can just jump. I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m following that thread for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, if that makes sense. But then the actual experiment, or the thing everyone needed from me, has sort of… fallen apart in the background. That’s really the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulties with distraction and focus when you are on vacation or completely away from work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Honestly, when I’m completely away from work, it’s harder for me to tell. I don’t think it shows up as dramatically on vacation, because there isn’t an experiment or a room full of people waiting for me to stay on track.\n\nWhat made me come in is really what happens at work—like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch on to something small, a pump vibration or a student’s offhand comment or even some old spectrum peak I suddenly remember, and then I’m off following that for hours. It feels important in the moment, not like I’m just drifting… but then the actual thing I was supposed to be focusing on has fallen apart.\n\nSo, I guess away from work it may happen in smaller ways, but I notice it most when there’s something concrete I’ve let slip. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have these problems with distraction and focus if you were to change jobs or work environments?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think a different environment would help… and maybe it would, if there were fewer interruptions or less pressure. But I’m not sure it would go away completely.\n\nWhat worries me is that it isn’t just this one job setup. I came in because I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Even in something structured, like a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum, and then I’m off chasing that for hours.\n\nIn the moment it feels relevant—like I’m being careful, not careless—but then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. So… I don’t know. I think changing jobs might make it less obvious, but I’m afraid the pattern would follow me. Does that sound reasonable?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with distraction and focus would still be present even if you were doing something you really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I wish I could say it only happens when I’m bored or stressed, but that wouldn’t be completely honest.\n\nA lot of the work I’m talking about is work I actually do enjoy. I like the experiments, I like noticing patterns, I like thinking through odd results. But that’s almost part of the problem — during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch on to something like a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll remember some strange peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nIn my head it feels productive, like I’m following something important. But then the actual experiment, or the thing people needed me to stay with, has fallen apart. So… yes, I think it would still be there, even with something I care about. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any effects of your distraction and focus issues on your personal relationships or friendships?\n\nPatient: I mean… personally, I’ve tried to keep it from spilling over too much. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming my relationships for work problems, or anything like that.\n\nBut I guess the honest answer is that it has affected people around me, especially at work. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle on my own, because I was missing priorities, mixing up scheduling, and not supervising as clearly as I should have. It got to the point where people were worried about the project and even the lab safety expectations.\n\nThat’s… embarrassing to say. I know you asked about friendships and relationships, and I’m not sure I have a neat answer there, but professionally, the relationships have definitely been strained. I don’t think people are trying to be unkind; I think they just can’t rely on me the same way right now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to manage your daily life outside of work due to these distractions and loss of focus?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say I’m managing okay outside of work, and in some ways I try to keep things looking normal. But… I think the clearest place it’s showing up is still work, because that’s where the consequences are harder to hide.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself. I’ve missed priorities, gotten scheduling confused, and my supervision hasn’t been as steady as it should be. It got serious enough that people were worried about the project and even the lab safety expectations.\n\nSo outside of work, I don’t know if I’d describe it as my whole daily life falling apart, but the focus problems are definitely affecting how I function. I’m sorry, is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But there are periods where I’m at the lab before anyone else gets there, and I’m rewriting protocols, rearranging the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in different countries, and starting new analyses all at once.\n\nIt feels productive at the time—like sitting still would be wasting this one chance to fix everything, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it acting without thinking… I mean, I do try to be responsible. Maybe I make decisions quickly sometimes, especially at work, but I usually have some reason in my head at the time.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what you mean by impulsive. Like, do you mean careless, or just moving too fast? I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're more prone to getting distracted when you're in a state of high energy or activity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s when it’s worse. When I’m in that high-energy mode, it feels like my mind is moving fast enough that every little thing seems important.\n\nLike I can be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll hear a pump vibrating differently, or a student makes some offhand comment, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m following that instead. At the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, but then hours have gone by and the actual experiment, or the thing people needed from me, has sort of fallen apart.\n\nI don’t mean to sound irresponsible. It’s just… yes, it does seem tied to when I’m more revved up.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you realize you've gotten off track and aren't meeting your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I feel… pretty anxious, honestly. I try not to show it, because I know everyone is busy and I don’t want to seem like I can’t handle my role.\n\nBut when I realize I’ve gone down the wrong path or missed what actually needed attention, it hits hard. My department has had to step in on some decisions I used to manage myself—scheduling, priorities, supervision things. I’d like to say it was just extra oversight, but… it was because things were getting confused, and there were concerns about the project and even the lab safety expectations.\n\nThat’s embarrassing to admit. I do care about the work. So yes, when I see that happening, I get very distressed. I worry I’ve let people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get distracted and have trouble focusing in your personal life as well, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly at work, I think… or at least that’s where it causes the most trouble, because the stakes are higher and people are waiting on me.\n\nAt home I can get a little scattered, but what really made me come in was that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I notice something like a pump vibration, or a student says something in passing, or I remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being diligent, you know? Like I’m following an important lead. But then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. That’s… that’s the part that worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your tendency to get distracted and lose focus is related to your mood or emotional state at the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is related. When I’m in that more elevated state—if that’s the right word—it almost feels like the lab is finally moving at my speed. Everything feels charged, like there are connections everywhere and I can see what needs to happen.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just being difficult, but… when someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I can get irritated. More than I’d like. I’ve snapped at people, and I think some of the students have started avoiding my office because of it.\n\nThat’s hard to say, because I don’t want to be that kind of person. But yes, the distraction and losing focus seem worse when I’m in that energized, keyed-up mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with distraction and focus at times when you're not at work or under pressure to meet deadlines?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly noticeable at work, if I’m being honest. That’s where it becomes obvious because there are meetings and experiments and people depending on me.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but even when it’s not exactly a deadline, like during a grant call or a safety meeting, my attention can just jump. I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum—and then I’m following that thread for hours.\n\nAt the time it feels like I’m being thorough, not distracted, if that makes sense. But then the actual experiment, or the thing everyone needed from me, has sort of… fallen apart in the background. That’s really the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulties with distraction and focus when you are on vacation or completely away from work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Honestly, when I’m completely away from work, it’s harder for me to tell. I don’t think it shows up as dramatically on vacation, because there isn’t an experiment or a room full of people waiting for me to stay on track.\n\nWhat made me come in is really what happens at work—like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch on to something small, a pump vibration or a student’s offhand comment or even some old spectrum peak I suddenly remember, and then I’m off following that for hours. It feels important in the moment, not like I’m just drifting… but then the actual thing I was supposed to be focusing on has fallen apart.\n\nSo, I guess away from work it may happen in smaller ways, but I notice it most when there’s something concrete I’ve let slip. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have these problems with distraction and focus if you were to change jobs or work environments?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think a different environment would help… and maybe it would, if there were fewer interruptions or less pressure. But I’m not sure it would go away completely.\n\nWhat worries me is that it isn’t just this one job setup. I came in because I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Even in something structured, like a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some odd peak from an old spectrum, and then I’m off chasing that for hours.\n\nIn the moment it feels relevant—like I’m being careful, not careless—but then the thing everyone actually needed from me has fallen apart. So… I don’t know. I think changing jobs might make it less obvious, but I’m afraid the pattern would follow me. Does that sound reasonable?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with distraction and focus would still be present even if you were doing something you really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I wish I could say it only happens when I’m bored or stressed, but that wouldn’t be completely honest.\n\nA lot of the work I’m talking about is work I actually do enjoy. I like the experiments, I like noticing patterns, I like thinking through odd results. But that’s almost part of the problem — during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch on to something like a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll remember some strange peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nIn my head it feels productive, like I’m following something important. But then the actual experiment, or the thing people needed me to stay with, has fallen apart. So… yes, I think it would still be there, even with something I care about. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any effects of your distraction and focus issues on your personal relationships or friendships?\n\nPatient: I mean… personally, I’ve tried to keep it from spilling over too much. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming my relationships for work problems, or anything like that.\n\nBut I guess the honest answer is that it has affected people around me, especially at work. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle on my own, because I was missing priorities, mixing up scheduling, and not supervising as clearly as I should have. It got to the point where people were worried about the project and even the lab safety expectations.\n\nThat’s… embarrassing to say. I know you asked about friendships and relationships, and I’m not sure I have a neat answer there, but professionally, the relationships have definitely been strained. I don’t think people are trying to be unkind; I think they just can’t rely on me the same way right now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to manage your daily life outside of work due to these distractions and loss of focus?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say I’m managing okay outside of work, and in some ways I try to keep things looking normal. But… I think the clearest place it’s showing up is still work, because that’s where the consequences are harder to hide.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself. I’ve missed priorities, gotten scheduling confused, and my supervision hasn’t been as steady as it should be. It got serious enough that people were worried about the project and even the lab safety expectations.\n\nSo outside of work, I don’t know if I’d describe it as my whole daily life falling apart, but the focus problems are definitely affecting how I function. I’m sorry, is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing difficulties in your personal relationships or friendships outside of work due to your distraction and focus issues?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to anything clearly outside of work. I don’t want to overstate it or make it sound worse than it is.\n\nWith friends, I’ve maybe been a little less present, but I don’t know that anyone has said, “This is a problem,” if that makes sense. I tend to keep things contained and just… not make plans if I’m worried I won’t be good company.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I haven’t really noticed a specific friendship or personal relationship breaking down because of it. The work side is much more obvious to me. Is that a fair way to answer?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
